Tesla Cybertruck Production in Texas Is Ending, Elon Musk Says, Truck Seems Likely to Be Killed Off

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The truck’s lead engineer is already referring to it in the past tense on X.

Tesla is “winding down” production of the Cybertruck in Texas “around the middle of next year” to make room for additional Cybercab production, CEO Elon Musk says. As with so many other Tesla lineup announcements, the decision was announced in a tweet shared overnight. The Cybertruck’s lead engineer followed up with an X post of his own referring to the vehicle in the past tense, suggesting that the pickup will be axed rather than see production moved elsewhere.

It marks a fitting end for what was meant to be a major volume product for Tesla, one that was intended to fight the Ford F-150 and Chevrolet Silverado the same way that the more successful Model 3 and Model Y have long fought internal combustion sedans and crossovers across the world. The unusually-styled Cybertruck never became anything close to that kind of rainmaker for Tesla, even after Tesla introduced a cheaper, more basic model last year. Those results made this outcome almost inevitable.

The Cybertruck was revealed in November of 2019, but not delivered to customers until November of 2023. When the truck did arrive, it failed to hit the lofty range and price benchmarks that Tesla announced on stage at its initial reveal. With production now set to end in the middle of 2027, the gap between the reveal and release could end up being longer than the amount of time Tesla actually sold its flagship pickup.

Cybertrucks were recalled seven times in 2024 alone and again throughout its life cycle, most notably for risks of unintended acceleration and entire pieces of bare metal bodywork that could be shed at speed. In 2025, one owner shared a video of that piece flying off his truck with Road & Track.

Without the Cybertruck out, the Roadster set for a claimed 2028 release, and the Model S and Model X canceled, Tesla’s only current products will soon be the Model 3 and Model Y. Both have been major hits for Tesla, and both have received recent facelifts, but they will have to carry the brand’s showrooms on their own until Tesla can actually find a way to deliver its promised Cybercab.

Fred Smith's love of cars comes from his fascination with auto racing. Unfortunately, that passion led him to daily drive a high-mileage, first-year Porsche Panamera. He is still thinking about the last lap of the 2011 Indianapolis 500.

What To Do If The Unlearn Button Is Missing In WoW: Forever

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft: Forever lets you learn up to two primary professions at a time, but you aren't stuck with your first choice. Maybe you picked Mining and Herbalism to earn gold selling materials at the auction house while leveling, only to decide later you'd rather craft your own gear or potions.

Fortunately, you can unlearn a profession to make room for a new one. Before switching, though, it's worth knowing where to find the option and what happens to your progress. We also encountered an issue with the unlearn button, but found a workaround that fixed it for us.

You don't need to visit a trainer to unlearn a profession.

Once you confirm, that profession is removed, and its slot becomes available. You can then visit a profession trainer to learn a new profession.

However, unlearning a profession resets your skill progress and removes the recipes you've unlocked. If you take up that profession again later, your previous progress won't return automatically, and you'll have to relearn its recipes. This applies to both purchased and looted recipes.

Only primary professions count toward the two-profession limit. Cooking, Fishing, and First Aid are secondary professions, and you can learn all three without using either primary slot.

A World of Warcraft Forever player has taken matters into their own hands after the flood of new Olympus guilds overseen by Asmongold.

We encountered an issue where the red X appeared as a blank space in the Professions window. The Unlearn tooltip appeared next to the blank space when we hovered over it, but we couldn't click the button. Changing the UI Scale in our game fixed the issue and let us unlearn professions again.

Unlearning a profession in World of Warcraft: Forever only takes a few steps, and adjusting the UI Scale may help if the red X isn't appearing. Just double-check your choice before confirming. You can always learn the profession again, but your previous skill progress and recipes won't automatically return.

Act 1 of the new World of Warcraft-themed Baldur's Gate 3 custom campaign, Forsaken, is available now.

22 Killer Watches I Saw at One of New York’s Most Exclusive Collector Hangs

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

This is an edition of the newsletter Box + Papers, Cam Wolf’s weekly deep dive into the world of watches. Sign up here.

I knew the watch collector meetup I was going to was legit when its organizer sent this text to the group: “We’ve engaged an off-duty NYPD detective to work security for our dinner. So, now really no reason not to bring your best!”

Last Wednesday night, at a fancy steakhouse in Midtown Manhattan, I was reminded why I love these enthusiast-organized events. This one was relatively small in comparison to blowouts like Rolliefest, but the idea was the same: A dozen or so collectors brought along their most prized timepieces and plunked them down on the table unattended (besides the off-duty NYPD detective, of course). When I asked one attendee if I could take his Breguet—similar to one heading to auction this weekend with a $73,000 estimate—across the room to take a photograph, he reacted like I’d asked if I could breathe oxygen: Of course! What a silly question. Events like these genuinely reflect the best of the hobby: deeply passionate collectors who really just want to share their watches with like-minded obsessives.

Between bites of miniature beef wellingtons and medium-rare steak, the guests passed around and ogled the dozens of grails on display. There were new classics on the table—including Vacheron Constantin’s Ultra-Thin, a leading contender for watch of the year—alongside plenty of old ones, including every flavor of vintage Rolex, from GMT-Masters to Daytonas to Explorer IIs. I was standing with one collector as he examined a pretty vintage Rolex GMT-Master. He stopped and looked around. So-and-so must be here, he muttered aloud. How did he know? Because he recognized the shade of the patina on the dial.

This particular group started getting together a couple of years ago, after the collectors @HorologicallyMinded, @s_h_lemon, @null_islands, and @jmsfisk (everyone at the event asked to remain anonymous) met at another watch-related retreat. @HorologicallyMinded, or HM, says that collecting was at one point something he did in solitude. “People generally judge when they learn how much watches cost,” HM tells me. “You paid how much for that!?” But he and the other three founders began meeting up for monthly dinners before eventually taking turns to organize these larger events. “This group has been the most rewarding part of this hobby,” HM says. “It’s been so great to have a group of people that have a common interest, but it’s not work-related and no one judges.”

Although the group has grown, the core four are still instrumental in making sure the functions go off without a hitch, with just the right spread of watches on the tables. “It is very deliberate and the four of us generally coordinate on it,” HM says. “We specifically make sure there is something for everyone.” That means ensuring there are at least a handful of watches worth less than $10,000—this time, that included several Rolex Datejusts, a Vulcain Cricket, and a gold Breitling from the 1940s. “We knew we were having some relative newbies and different people have different budgets, and we want everyone to be comfortable.” On the other end of the spectrum, some grail-level pieces were special requests from other members of the group.

All the watches wound up getting plenty of TLC. “I love the fact that we’re a group that can, and did, totally geek out about a vintage Longines Legend Diver, while a Doré dial Patek is on the same table,” HM says.

Below are some of the very best pieces I saw that night.

A Patek Philippe ref. 3970, part of the house’s historic lineage of pieces that combine a perpetual calendar with a chronograph. The dial on this one is so tasty.

A skeletonized Breguet Ref. 3355PT00PA0—maybe the watch of the night for me—on a show-stopping platinum bracelet.

A few years ago, MB&F started making more approachable versions of its sci-fi-ready watches and allocating them through a lottery system. This is the M.A.D2, with an outer ring on the dial that spins around very quickly. It’s really fun.

The aforementioned Rolex GMT-Master is a ref. 16760, a model produced during the ’80s that’s nicknamed the “Sophia Loren” thanks to its curvaceous profile.

Omega has incredible stuff in its archives. This is a chronograph from the ’30s that’s controlled entirely through that single button at 2 o’clock.

A couple of real bangers here. The Vacheron on the left is known as the “Cioccolatone,” because it resembles a square of chocolate. At the center is the Patek Philippe ref. 3417, an antimagnetic watch with just about the prettiest typography you’ll ever see on a watch. Zohran Mamdani should adopt that cursive for signs in the city, because it can make just about anything look nice: Warning: Rats. Or maybe, No Dumping Here.

A cloisonné dial from—of all brands—IWC! I am such a sucker for a cloisonné, which refers to the intricate images carefully fashioned from wire and colorful baked enamel.

A beautiful example of one of the Seafarers that Heuer made for Abercrombie & Fitch from the ’50s through the ’70s, back when the latter was still a rugged outdoor outfitter. The shades of orange, yellow, and blue are dreamy.

A monochromatic take on the Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Perpetual Calendar, which was produced in the ’80s and ’90s but looks incredibly modern.

Universal Genève finally relaunched last year after decades on the shelf, but it’s hard to beat the originals. This Polerouter comes on an extremely cool “wave” bracelet, which basically uses a repeating chevron pattern to make the slinky bit of metal appear to be in motion.

During World War II, a dozen watch manufacturers came together to make models following a strict set of specifications for the British Ministry of Defence. One of those brands was Longines, which produced this strapping piece.

You didn’t think we’d wrap this list up without a gorgeous yellow-gold Daytona, did you?

The ultimate fusion of sport and luxury watches. Heuer supplied this ’70s-era Carrera to Ferrari’s racing team, including legendary drivers like Jacky Ickx, Niki Lauda, and Clay Regazzoni.

This Patek is cool, but the bracelet is insane.

This isn’t your average Patek Philippe Nautilus. PP began producing this reference 3800 in 1981 as a smaller counterpart to the original. The few times I’ve gotten to try one on, I’m always struck by how perfect the proportions are. It’s nice and thin and looks just right on the wrist.

The United States Merchant Marines’ logo really punches up this Day-Date.

The ref. 6541 is the most beloved version of the Rolex Milgauss. It was produced in the ’50s and features a signature honeycomb dial. One sold for $2.5 million at auction in 2023.

Is there a better way to time your yacht race than with this Heuer Skipper? Probably not.

It’s good to have doubles, especially when it comes to vintage Rolex Explorer IIs.

Liverpool looking at one of Ruben Amorim’s top players at AC Milan – report

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Liverpool are reportedly paying close attention to AC Milan centre-back Koni De Winter.

That’s the word coming from CaughtOffside’s Mark Brus on The Daily Briefing, with Manchester United and Chelsea also said to be among the defender’s potential suitors.

The three big-hitters in question aren’t the only Premier League clubs keeping a watching brief on the No.5.

So there’s a distinct possibility that De Winter could be making his move to the English top-flight sooner rather than later, if he can maintain his current performance levels.

From Liverpool’s perspective, interest in signing a new centre-back is more than understandable given question marks over the futures of Joe Gomez and Virgil van Dijk. Meanwhile, it’s not quite clear whether loan signing Ronald Araujo’s move to Anfield will be made permanent in light of his superb form in the 2026/27 season.

Either way, the Reds will require at least one new centre-half come the opening of the summer transfer window.

One sticking point for Liverpool might be Milan and Ruben Amorim’s use of the Belgian star in a three-man central defence.

But looking past that, De Winter has posted some reasonable numbers since his move to the Italian giants last year.

It will certainly be encouraging for Andoni Iraola that the young centre-half ranks well for line-breaking passes and aerial duels, particularly with the club’s progressive capabilities yet to fully recover in a post-Trent Alexander-Arnold world.

* Koni De Winter’s stats for AC Milan in the 2025/26 Serie A season (FotMob)

At 24 years of age, however, it would seem that the former Juventus star is still very much a work in progress. Of course, this will no doubt be welcomed by a coach with Iraola’s appetite for development.

And adding another centre-back option to the ranks who’s capable of getting the ball out of the backline is something we’re far from opposed to.

Earth's Gravity May Have Kept the Early Moon's Volcanic Heart Beating

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

We typically think of the Moon as a static, floating object that has remained the same for basically its entire lifetime, with an occasional asteroid impact serving as an exception. That understanding is grounded in physics - the Moon should have cooled rapidly after forming when a huge protoplanet hit the early Earth around 4.5 billion years ago. But, some lunar material returned from the Changâe-5 mission was notably formed only around two billion years ago, yet appeared to be volcanic in origin. So how did a world that was supposed to have settled down without volcanism remain hot enough to create these materials more than two billion years after its violent birth? A new paper from Miao Chen of the Chengdu University of Technology and his colleagues, and recently published in the journal Planet, offers an explanation - tidal heating.

Letâs first set the stage of the Moonâs creation. Our best guess is that it was created when another early protoplanet, named Theia, slammed into Earth in a massive collision, creating a chunk of debris that coalesced into the Moon. However, that amount of debris was relatively small - only about a quarter the size of the Earth itself, which meant the internal heat generated during its formation would rapidly radiate away, turning the Moon itself geologically dormant.

But when Changâe-5 returned some lunar basalts from Oceanus Procellarum back to Earth, laboratory dating showed them to be only around two billion years old. Basalts are only formed in active volcanoes, or other environments with similarly high pressures and temperatures. Environments that, at least according to traditional geological theory, shouldnât have existed on the Moon two billion years ago.

Fraser explains why the Moon is moving farther away.

Despite that, scientists have tried offering other solutions. Radioactive elements, such as potassium and uranium, emit some heat as they decay. But there wasnât enough un-decayed material left on the Moon to offer the amount of heat needed to form basalt two billion years ago. Asteroid strikes could cause localized high impact thermal spikes, but the heavy bombardment period that gave the Moon most of its craters had ended long before the basalt was formed.

So instead the authors turned to gravity - specifically to a phenomenon called tidal heating. This happens when a celestial body is squeezed, stretched, and kneaded as it travels in an elliptical orbit around a larger parent body. Perhaps the most famous example of this in the solar system is Io. This moon of Jupiter is constantly erupting due to the tidal squishing it undergoes during its orbit around the largest planet.

Obviously, the Moon doesnât seem to be undergoing any such squishing currently. However, two billion years ago, the Moon was much closer to the Earth than it is today, orbiting our planet in sixteen days rather than the twenty seven it takes now. Its orbit was also much more eccentric, looking much more like an oval than a circle. All of which means that Earth itself exerted huge rhythmic tidal forces on the Moon' s interior during the course of its orbit.

Fraser details Tidal Locking, which also affects the Moons geology.

Weâve known that for awhile, but astronomers have always modeled the Moon as a mostly uniform sphere when trying to understand how these gravitational squeezes affected it. However, scientists knew this was a simplification - there is an ultra-low viscosity zone of rock wrapping itself between the Moonâs core and mantle. When the research team plugged a more realistic 3D representation of the mantle setup into a convection simulation, they noticed that a ton of energy was directed specifically at two symmetric regions along the lunar core-mantle boundary.

These regions, which they named Tidal Heating Anomaly Zones (THAZs) , absorbed a huge amount of energy. They calculated that, around 3.2 billion years ago, despite taking up only 2.5% of the mantleâs total volume, the tidal strain on these regions delivered 150 gigawatts of power - more than four times the amount of energy generated by radioactive decay.

This seemed to be the driving force behind much of the Moonâs geological history. It helped keep the lunar core warm, which helped sustain a magnetic field for much longer the models originally predicted (and was a puzzle when scientists realized that Apollo lunar samples seemed to have formed in a strong magnetic field). It also drove huge mantle plumes that swelled toward the crust. Billions of years later, long after the tidal engine has calmed down, those stranded pockets of mantle melted to produce the basalts seen in the Changâe-5 samples.

If thereâs one thing thatâs clear from the paperâs theory, it's that we still donât know a lot about the Moonâs geological history. This new model offers some elegant answers to some of the most persistent questions in that area, but as of now itâs still only a theory. Maybe some of the numerous lunar missions planned over the next decade will be able to provide more definitive data to finally solve the evolutionary questions of our nearest neighbor.

Higher Education Press / EurekAlert - Could tides have concentrated heat deep inside the moon? A new model reveals a possible heat source

M. Chen, D. Wang, & Y. Liu - Tidal Heating Anomaly Zone at the core-mantle boundary of the Moon

UT - A Buried Volcano on the Moon Hints At An Ancient Lunar Dynamo

UT - The Moon had Volcanoes More Recently Than Previously Believed

Andy has been interested in space exploration ever since reading Pale Blue Dot in middle school. An engineer by training, he likes to focus on the practical challenges of space exploration, whether that's getting rid of perchlorates on Mars or making ultra-smooth mirrors to capture ever clearer data. When not writing or engineering things he can be found entertaining his four children, four cats, and two dogs, or running in circles and throwing plastic discs at people to stay in shape.

Scriptable GeoDNS: leaving AWS for Bunny

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

To op­ti­mize the per­for­mance met­rics of this blog, I re­lied on Ama­zon Cloud­Front to serve the as­sets,1 and Route 53 for the geo-aware DNS2 to di­rect read­ers to the clos­est host serv­ing the con­tent. But Route 53 is ex­pen­sive: US$0.50 per zone per month, US$0.70 per mil­lion ge­olo­ca­tion queries, and US$0.75 per healthcheck per month[1], plus US$1 per month for the KMS key[2] needed to sign zones with DNSSEC. When I dis­cov­ered that Bunny DNS is al­most free[3] for up to 500 do­mains and fea­tures script­able an­swers, I made the jump! Plus, it’s a Eu­ro­pean com­pany based in Slovenia.

I had been host­ing me­dia as­sets on Cloud­Front since 2011. ❦

Route 53 had served bernat.ch since De­cem­ber 2021. I didn’t use healthchecks as they were too ex­pen­sive. I didn’t host most of my zones there ei­ther, as queries for haproxy.debian.net alone would have cost me around US$10 per month. ❦

Bunny.net[4] started as BunnyCDN in 2015[5]. They added IPv6 sup­port in 2017[6]. Switch­ing was un­event­ful. I don’t bring a cus­tom do­main: I serve the con­tent from the b-cdn.net do­main. As I de­pend on con­tent ne­go­ti­a­tion to serve AVIF and WebP im­ages[7], I con­fig­ure the CDN to cache each im­age for­mat sep­arately[8].

I dis­able log­ging[9] to avoid col­lect­ing per­sonal in­for­ma­tion. While the bunny.net GDPR page[10] prom­ises they anonymize data, it is un­clear what Cloud­Front keeps in its own logs when log­ging is disabled.

The only down­side of the bunny.net CDN of­fer­ing is the lack of HTTP/3. I asked their sup­port: two min­utes later, they an­swered they are work­ing on it but have no ETA to share.

Bunny.net in­tro­duced their DNS of­fer­ing in 2022[11] and re­cently re­duced its cost to al­most zero[3]. They sup­port ge­o­graph­i­cal rout­ing[12], but a very cool fea­ture is the script­able DNS[13]: it runs a piece of user-sup­plied Java­Script on each query to com­pute the answer.

I run seven small NixOS vir­tual ma­chines to host this blog around the world: Paris, Nurem­berg, Ash­burn, Sin­ga­pore, San Jose, São Paulo, and Syd­ney. Bunny’s script­able DNS lets me re­turn the IP ad­dresses of the two near­est servers. The browser falls back to the sec­ond server if the first one is down.

The above script re­lies on the RoutingEngine.getClosestServer()[14] helper, which re­turns the clos­est alive3 server to the user.4 Thanks to Doggo[15], we can check it works as ex­pected, at least for A:5

A server is alive if it an­swers an ICMP ping. The bunny.net doc­u­men­ta­tion[16] gives more de­tails about the checks. ❦

The user queries Bunny DNS through a re­solver. Usu­ally, ei­ther the re­solver is close to the user, or it ex­poses the user’s sub­net through EDNS client subnet[17]. ❦

Don’t use this script as is: RoutingEngine.getClosestServer() only works with IPv4. The fi­nal script[18] works around this lim­i­tation. ❦

The most an­noy­ing draw­back of this method is that a user from Buenos Aires gets both São Paulo (1000 mi, 30 ms RTT) and Ash­burn (5200 mi, 140 ms RTT). Most re­solvers ran­dom­ize the or­der, so the user may pull the web page from Ash­burn. We could re­turn only the near­est server—and swap the scripted record with the built-in ge­o­graphic rout­ing in­stead. But if the server be­comes un­avail­able, the browser has no al­ter­na­tive un­til the DNS cache ex­pires. It leaves a short win­dow of un­avail­abil­ity, even with a short TTL.

The HTTPS DNS record[19], de­fined in RFC 9460[20], solves both the avail­abil­ity and the la­tency is­sues. Mod­ern browsers query this record to get ad­di­tional in­for­ma­tion to reach an HTTP server, in­clud­ing an al­ter­na­tive port and the ap­pli­ca­tion layer protocol—HTTP/2 or HTTP/3. Each record has a pri­or­ity. We can ask browsers to con­tact the near­est server first and fall back to the next one if the first is un­available:

This looks like the ideal so­lu­tion! Un­for­tu­nately, Chrome ig­nores any record whose tar­get is not .[21], hence the last record at pri­or­ity 3. More­over, un­like with a CNAME, Bunny DNS does not re­turn the IP ad­dresses for first.geo and second.geo in the ad­di­tional sec­tion. The re­solver needs to send a first re­quest to get the HTTPS record, and a sec­ond to re­solve first.geo.

HTTPS records are still too new[22] for this use case. Back to the draw­ing board! 📐

As a com­pro­mise, the script re­turns the two near­est servers if they are at a sim­i­lar dis­tance from the user. Oth­er­wise, it re­turns only the near­est one with a lower TTL of 2 minutes.

We can check it with Doggo. From Chicago, we get only the Ash­burn server with a TTL of 2 min­utes, while from Mex­ico, we get both Ash­burn and San Jose.

On the live page[23], the map be­low is in­ter­ac­tive: point at a lo­ca­tion to get the DNS an­swer, or dis­able servers to see how the an­swers change.

En­able Java­Script to make the map be­low in­ter­ac­tive: point at a lo­ca­tion to get the DNS an­swer, or dis­able servers to see how the an­swers change.

This page was brought to you by ???. Check on the map be­low whether it matches your location!

The com­plete code[18] is a bit more com­plex. It works around RoutingEngine.getClosestServer() sup­port­ing only IPv4, and it han­dles the case where there are not enough alive servers to build an answer.

Bunny.net has a com­pre­hen­sive Ter­raform provider[24]. I lever­age CDK Ter­rain[25] to build the tar­get state in Type­Script and OpenTofu[26] to de­ploy it. Have a look at “Man­ag­ing in­fra­struc­ture with Ter­raform, CDKTF, and NixOS[27]” for more details.6

The ar­ti­cle is a bit out­dated. CDK Ter­rain re­places CDKTF, OpenTofu re­places Ter­raform, and I now use Col­mena[28] in­stead of NixOps. ❦

Route 53 cov­ered one ad­di­tional use case of mine: serv­ing acme.luffy.cx to solve DNS-01 ACME chal­lenges[29]. Un­like Route 53, Bunny DNS can­not re­strict ac­cess to a sin­gle zone with a fine-grained ac­cess pol­icy. As this us­age re­quires nei­ther high avail­abil­ity nor low la­tency, I re­placed Route 53 with Hick­ory[30], a mem­ory-safe DNS server writ­ten in Rust. It sup­ports au­to­matic DNSSEC sig­na­tures[31] and dy­namic up­dates[32]. Its con­fig­u­ra­tion is simple:

The pro­vided zone file[33] only seeds the data­base on first start. I del­e­gate DNS-01 chal­lenges to this zone with CNAME records:

Lego[34], the ACME client I use to get cer­tifi­cates from Let’s En­crypt[35], an­swers the DNS-01 chal­lenge by push­ing en­tries with the DNS UPDATE protocol[36].

Un­for­tu­nately, Bunny DNS can­not pub­lish a DS record in a zone.7 I need one to se­cure the acme.luffy.cx zone and cer­tifi­cate de­liv­ery. There­fore, I host the luffy.cx zone on deSEC[37],8 a free DNS host­ing ser­vice run by a non-profit or­ga­ni­za­tion based in Berlin. I de­rive the DS record to pub­lish in luffy.cx from the DNSKEY record of acme.luffy.cx:

I con­tacted bunny.net sup­port to ask when they will sup­port pub­lish­ing a DS record and got an an­swer seven min­utes later: they don’t know. 😭

This is a bum­mer, as they sup­port au­to­matic CDS, a DNS record to ad­ver­tise a DS record to the par­ent zone. They han­dle one side of the del­e­ga­tion, not the other.

I also re­ported a bug with the proof of non-ex­is­tence, and they replied a day later with a de­tailed answer. ❦

Why not use deSEC for ACME chal­lenges? You can limit its to­kens to a sin­gle RRset[38]. I could say I pre­fer to self-host, but the truth is I only dis­cov­ered this while writ­ing this article. ❦

Over­all, I am quite happy with the switch to bunny.net. There are still a few rough edges, like the in­abil­ity to pub­lish DS records, the lack of HTTP/3, and the IPv4-only RoutingEngine.getClosestServer() helper. The bill went from US$5 per month to US$1. So far, per­for­mance is on par with AWS[39], but I only fi­nal­ized the cur­rent setup, in­clud­ing the server in Syd­ney, in late Oc­to­ber. I need a bit more time to gather more data, and I’ll post a short update. 📈

This post was briefly dis­cussed on Hacker News[40].

A Scottish influence has transformed New Zealand – but can the Scots finally beat the All Blacks?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The New Zealand head coach Dave Rennie took plenty from his three years at the Glasgow Warriors but it is the chuckles that he remembers most fondly. "The Scottish are funny people and I haven't laughed as much in any environment as much as I have in my time here," Rennie explained back in 2020 after embarking on what proved an ill-fated stint with Australia. "I've loved the people and I've loved our time here. It's been special, we'll miss it."

Dave Rennie spent three years at Glasgow (Getty)

Perhaps it is no surprise, then, that there has been such a strong Scottish influence at the heart of his project to restore the All Blacks' spirit. Mike Blair won 85 caps at scrum half and captained Scotland 14 times; until deciding he rather preferred life overseeing attack, it appeared he was being groomed as Gregor Townsend's successor in the highest coaching office in the land. Instead, he and Rennie return to Murrayfield with a New Zealand side in a phase of reinvention or rediscovery – re-finding the tenets that made them great and forging perhaps the world's most outstanding attack.

It has proved a natural fit. Blair has admitted to feeling a degree of impostor syndrome when he first came into All Blacks camp earlier this year, but has found the squad highly receptive to his methods. He has done excellent work, too, getting a tune out of a series of playmakers and associated parts through a demanding itinerary. Unbeaten so far in the Nations Championship having overcome France, Italy and Ireland in July, they carry confidence from two professional performances against the Wallabies last month.

"We're in a good place, I think," Blair said this week. "We're pleased with what we did against Australia but we're also aware that playing Scotland at Murrayfield is a really big challenge. It's another good opportunity to show the growth in our game."

Scotland struggled to get close to New Zealand during the playing days of Mike Blair (far left) (Getty)

The former scrum half made a self-deprecating crack that he'd already contributed to a few All Blacks wins over Scotland this week but his own playing days do serve as a reminder of how this challenge has changed for the tourists. Back when their attack coach wore the thistle, the result was a foregone conclusion; in four meetings with the All Blacks as a player, Blair only got within 20 points once. "When I played, New Zealand played their second-string team. They gave guys opportunity against us," he admitted. "Times have changed from that." The last four meetings have been decided by eight points or fewer.

Scotland will rue missed opportunities in at least two of those, including last year – having hauled the game back to level pegging from 17-0 down, Townsend's side could not sustain their effort. One could feel echoes of that game during a not dissimilar defeat to South Africa during the first phase of the Nations Championship in July – this tournament may have its critics but in ensuring a regularity of fixture against the very top teams, it is of great benefit to Scotland.

Will that translate into the next step of finally beating New Zealand? The fear of some is that last year represented a significant missed opportunity against a team in flux. While consistency and coherence in selection puts Scotland in a good place, they will be at a disadvantage in starting a campaign against an international team that has been together virtually since July. No doubt, though, that Townsend will want his team to try to match their expressive visitors stride-for-stride.

Damian McKenzie's try helped New Zealand beat Scotland in a Murrayfield thriller last year (PA Wire)

"It's obviously an exciting occasion getting to play the All Blacks," said the lock Scott Cummings. "The build-up probably feels a bit different this time because the last couple of times we've played them it's been three or four years (since the previous meeting) but we got to play them last year, so we know what to expect from them. Obviously they're a quality outfit and we know we need to be at our best on the day to beat them. I believe we can do that."

Confidence will be taken from the strong start enjoyed in the United Rugby Championship by both Scottish sides, with Edinburgh joining Glasgow among the early pace-setters. Townsend has largely put trust in those who have served him well before, but the form of Liam McConnell, Freddy Douglas and others deepens the pool the options. They may be required in a busy November.

Those rigours will be felt, too, by a New Zealand squad entering the final stretch of a gruelling international itinerary that began at the start of July. This has been a tough week, too, after the sudden passing of security chief Logan Cadwallader in Edinburgh. The All Blacks will carry his memory on to the field. "Logan has been a big part of the All Blacks family for a long time," Rennie said. "He was the ultimate team man, loved and trusted by all and a true professional. He looked after all of us, players and staff, and he was a good mate to many in this team. We are all devastated. As a team, we will honour Logan and the legacy he has left on the All Blacks."

8 Episodes Of Netflix’s Greatest Sci-Fi Hit Deliver Peak Lovecraftian Horror

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Reading Lovecraftian horror feels like reading an ancient forbidden text. There is a timelessness to H.P. Lovecraft's display of the "fear of the unknown" that transcends the page and immerses one into complete existential dread. Yet, despite feeling ancient and timeless, Lovecraftian horror rarely translates well to the screen.

There are not even a handful of worthy direct adaptations of H.P. Lovecraft's stories. Quite a few cosmic horror movies and shows, however, have succeeded at loosely drawing from his works. One of them is Netflix's greatest sci-fi hit, Stranger Things. To clarify, Stranger Things, as a whole, is not a Lovecraftian horror show. It is arguably not even horror.

However, in its first 8 episodes, Stranger Things draws heavily from Lovecraft's chilling Eldritch lore and his ambiguous descriptions of monsters and ancient gods. Lovecraftian horror is certainly one of the many literary influences that ultimately shaped Stranger Things. However, the Netflix sci-fi show's heavy inclination towards cosmic horror in season 1 just cannot be ignored.

The origins of cosmic terrors in every Lovecraftian story stem from humanity's hubris. For instance, in H.P. Lovecraft's seminal short story From Beyond, an ambitious scientist makes an electronic device that allows humans to perceive what lies beyond our scope of accepted reality. With this, he unknowingly allows interdimensional creatures from alternate dimensions to perceive and manipulate humans.

Something very similar unfolds in Stranger Things season 1.

Dr. Martin Brenner and Hawkins National Laboratory open "The Gate" in an attempt to "expand" human consciousness and weaponize it to spy on the Soviets. Little do they realize that they have exposed humanity to an unknown dimension. Just like the terrifying co-existing but hidden dimensions in Lovecraftian horror, Stranger Things' "Upside Down" is not merely hell ruled by the devil.

Instead, it is more of an ancient and toxic "non-Euclidean" plane that mirrors Hawkins. Just like the sunken city of R'lyeh from The Call of Cthulhu, even the "Upside Down" is cold, dark, endless, and incomprehensible.

Almost throughout its run, Stranger Things season 1 also avoids revealing the true nature or form of the Demogorgon. It is packed with horrifying ambiguous imagery where Will attempts to communicate with his family from the "other side" while struggling to escape the monster's grasp.

Similar to Lovecraftian protagonists, even Stranger Things characters like Joyce feel so helpless against the cosmic indifference of their world that they start losing their minds.

In its final arc, Stranger Things season 1 also gives viewers and its characters an illusion of control. It establishes that the power of friendship seemingly saves the day and Will and his family live happily ever after. But just before Stranger Things season 1 ends, Will realizes that control and autonomy were mere illusions. With this, Stranger Things subtly mocks human authority just like Lovecraftian stories.

The Duffer Brothers have previously cited H.P. Lovecraft's stories as an influence on Stranger Things (via EW).

Going into season 2, Stranger Things manages to retain some of its cosmic horror aspects. However, at the same time, it makes its terrors and scares a little too "tangible." This marks the show's departure from cosmic horror. As it progresses, Stranger Things bends genres and even covers coming-of-age themes in latter seasons.

Towards the end, the Netflix sci-fi show feels more like a conventional supernatural thriller than a groundbreaking take on Lovecraftian horror tropes. Perhaps, this explains why, in hindsight, most viewers are kinder towards Stranger Things' earlier chapters and more harsh when it comes to its divisive season 5.

Stranger Things is a Netflix series set in the 1980s, where the mysterious disappearance of a young boy leads a small town to uncover secret experiments and supernatural forces.

A Knight Of The Seven Kingdoms Officially Returns December 16

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

After HBO's divisive House of the Dragon adaptation, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms feels like exactly the change of pace George R.R. Martin's fantasy universe needed. Based on his beloved Dunk and Egg novellas, the series shifts focus off of dragons and political intrigue to a more intimate journey across Westeros, offering fans a refreshing perspective on the world they've come to know and love.

For longtime A Song of Ice and Fire readers, there's something especially satisfying about seeing this corner of Martin's universe finally receive its own live-action adaptation. The stories of Ser Duncan the Tall and his young squire, Egg, have always stood apart from the main series, exploring the everyday lives, traditions, and struggles of Westeros through the eyes of characters far removed from the usual political elite.

In many ways, the show's smaller scale feels like a welcome return to what made Game of Thrones so compelling in the first place, with richly developed characters, unpredictable encounters, and a world that feels genuinely lived in. Fortunately, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms is returning this December with a brand-new release (via SFHobby).

A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms fans have a major release to look forward to before the end of 2026, with Rittenhouse Archives officially launching its Season One Trading Card collection on December 16, 2026. The upcoming set celebrates HBO's acclaimed Game of Thrones prequel, featuring collectible cards highlighting the characters, memorable moments, and performances that made Dunk and Egg's first adventure together so compelling.

According to the official Rittenhouse Archives listing, the collection features a 46-card base set, comprising 18 episode cards (three for each of Season 1's six episodes) and 28 character cards. However, there are plenty of additional collectibles to chase, including several limited-edition variants and specialty inserts.

Probably the best part of the new set, however, is the collection's assortment of autographed cards, with three included in every box. These feature genuine on-card signatures from the show's cast, with a partial list of confirmed signers including Peter Claffey (Dunk), Dexter Sol Ansell (Egg), Finn Bennett (Aerion Targaryen), Daniel Ings (Lyonel Baratheon), Sam Spruell (Maekar Targaryen), and Danny Webb (Ser Arlan of Pennytree).

There's also the "A Tall Tale That Became a Legend" case-topper card, while an exclusive 10-case incentive box features additional autograph cards, printing plates, and other collectibles. Individual boxes are currently listed at $199.99, while a companion binder costs $29.99. Rittenhouse notes that the collection's contents are still subject to change.

Of course, fans won't have to wait much longer for Dunk and Egg's next televised adventure, either. HBO has already confirmed that A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms Season 2 will arrive during the first half of 2027, although an exact premiere date hasn't been announced. The next season will adapt George R.R. Martin's second Dunk and Egg novella, The Sworn Sword, continuing the unlikely duo's adventures across Westeros.

Walter Koenig Had A Hilarious Explanation For Star Trek’s Infamous Khan Plot Hole

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan has one of the franchise's most infamous plot holes, but Walter Koenig hilariously explained it away.

Walter Koenig passed away at 90 on October 25, 2026. Koenig's finest dramatic performance in Star Trek was in director Nicholas Meyer's Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan in 1982, when the supervillain Khan (Ricardo Montalban) tortured and took control of Commander Pavel Chekov's mind. There was just one problem the Star Trek fans have pointed out for 44 years: Khan claimed he never forgot Chekov's face, but he never met the Russian Starfleet Officer in Star Trek: The Original Series.

The Nacelle Company released their full 90-minute interview with Walter Koenig that they conducted for The Center Seat II: 60 Years of Star Trek. Regarding Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan's plot hole, Koenig shared his tongue-in-cheek explanation that Ensign Chekov was in Star Trek: The Original Series' "Space Seed" and encountered Khan off-screen. Read Walter's quote below:

Walter Koenig: “So I made up a story. I made up a story how Chekov was in the bathroom, and he was so sick with malapropski's malady that he couldn’t get off the john. And Khan was on the other side banging pitifully on the door because he needed to relieve his bladder. Finally, when I came out, he looked at me and said, ‘Your face I will never forget.’”

Watch Walter Koenig's full interview for The Center Seat II: 60 Years of Star Trek:

Khan's Star Trek: The Original Series episode happened in season 1, while Ensign Chekov didn't join the show until season 2. Khan was stranded on Ceti Alpha V for nearly 20 years, suffering through the destruction of the neighboring Ceti Alpha V, which reduced his world to a barren wasteland and killed Khan's wife, Lt. Marla McGivers (Madlyn Rhue).

The full story of what happened on Ceti Alpha V was revealed in the 2025 podcast, Star Trek: Khan.

Director Nicholas Meyer's explanation for why Khan recognized Chekov is simpler than Koenig's: the director didn't know. When he accepted the job as Star Trek II's director, Meyer wasn't a Star Trek fan, to begin with. Nicholas gave himself a crash course on Star Trek as he wrote the script for The Wrath of Khan (which he originally titled Star Trek II: The Undiscovered Country), and the Chekov/Khan detail slipped past him.

Koenig said he raised the plot hole about Chekov and Khan during Star Trek II's production, but they went ahead and filmed the moment where Khan said he recognized Chekov regardless. It may not be canonically accurate, but the moment has undeniable dramatic power, as Khan used Chekov as a surrogate for his master plan of vengeance against Admiral James T. Kirk (William Shatner).

Screen Rant interviews Walter Koenig about his joining The 7th Rule podcast to review Star Trek: The Original Series, his Trek memories, and more.

Walter Koenig was a talented writer and nimble, witty storyteller who penned an episode of Star Trek: The Animated Series, so he has naturally concocted his 'Khan did meet Chekov' story that he has told often over the years, changing details occasionally, like "Chekov was trapped in the boiler room" when Khan took over the Starship Enterprise in "Space Seed." Koenig also reviewed Star Trek II on his podcast, The 7th Rule.

Rewatching Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan is extra fun when one weaves in Walter Koenig's explanation for how Khan recognized Mr. Chekov on Ceti Alpha V as head canon.

Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan is streaming on Paramount+

'Yellowstone' Killed Off One of Its Best Characters Too Early

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Writer and director Taylor Sheridan has, simply put, taken over our TV screens. The Jeremy Renner-led Mayor of Kingstown is gearing up for its fifth and final season, Lioness is still going strong, and Tulsa King, starring Sylvester Stallone, is currently airing its fourth installment with a spin-off on the way. None of these shows, however, would likely be in existence if it weren't for the massive success of Taylor Sheridan's Yellowstone. While the Western series, led by Kevin Costner, came to a close with Season 5, the Dutton dynasty is still being represented on television through CBS's Marshals and Paramount+'s Dutton Ranch.

While it can be hard for a multi-season series to remember every plotline from its early years — especially given how long Yellowstone's episodes can run — one forgotten character stands above the rest, awaiting real justice for his absence. With spin-off prequels such as 1883 and 1923 introducing so many other Duttons to add to the growing family tree, it's easy to forget about Dave Annable's Lee entirely. After all, the character isn't in the show all that much, and his siblings, Kayce (Luke Grimes), Beth (Kelly Reilly), and adoptive brother Jamie (Wes Bentley), often fought (usually each other) for Yellowstone's center stage. A humble cowboy like Lee wouldn't really fit into the cutthroat world Sheridan created, hence why he didn't stick around. But what happened to Lee, and why did Annable leave the show so abruptly?

If you forgot that Costner's John Dutton had a third son (well, fourth if you include Cole Hauser's Rip Wheeler, John's surrogate son), then you're not alone. Annable only played the firstborn Dutton in three episodes of Yellowstone, and in that time, his character never exactly made a real impact on anyone involved. First appearing in the extended pilot episode "Daybreak," Lee is the first in line for the Dutton throne. More than that, he's the only one of John's children who genuinely cares about the land and cattle, not just the power it buys. But Lee's tenure on the series wasn't meant to last, and neither was his memory.

Near the end of "Daybreak," Lee, himself a Livestock Agent, is murdered by Robert Long (Jeremiah Bitsui) — who just so happens to be Monica Dutton's (Kelsey Asbille) brother — during a cattle dispute on the border of the Dutton Ranch and the Broken Rock Indian Reservation. As a result, Kayce (Luke Grimes) retaliates by killing his brother-in-law in self-defense, which ends up causing more issues than Lee's death did. In many ways, Lee's demise serves as the catalyst for the events of the entire series, as the Duttons go to war with all the encroaching forces on their ancestral land. However, they sort of forget about him in the process.

The hidden meanings behind Beth's wounds might surprise you.

After his death, Annable was understandably written off the series. For years, his only reappearance was via a photo that cameoed in the Season 2 episode ironically titled "Resurrection Day." However, Lee did return via flashbacks to his sibling's childhood in the '90s (back when Costner's John Dutton looked more like Josh Lucas), where he was played by Kip Denton — who, aside from the episode "Blood the Boy," often went uncredited.

While Lee's occasional flashback appearances kept the character alive after his Season 1 demise (his pure moment with a young Kayce at the Dutton family Christmas of 1996 was a nice surprise), they're scarce and never focus on the character himself. In the present storylines, Lee's death is forgotten by the end of the first season, and rather than fight for the Dutton Ranch in honor of his son's memory, John Dutton does so out of some mysterious promise to his father that isn't revealed until later. By some miracle, Lee's the only Dutton child who's been murdered thus far, in spite of previous attempts on other family members' lives.

No doubt part of the reason that Lee is forgotten is that the death of a son and a brother is a hard thing to meditate on. No wonder John would rather put all his effort into preserving his land rather than grieve. But what starts as something understandable becomes something less so as time continues on. As Yellowstone continues to flashback to the Duttons' childhood, Lee is forgotten about entirely. Additionally, there are entire Dutton family photos that don't include their firstborn son at all, which we'd assume was a point of contention among the siblings if we didn't know it was clearly unintentional. More than anything, it just seems like Taylor Sheridan forgot about Lee's existence completely.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

In many ways, Yellowstone developed Rip into Lee's replacement, especially in recent seasons. Not only did John gift his surrogate firstborn Lee's old cabin on the Dutton land, but John's love for his new son-in-law rivaled that of his love for his own children. Flashbacks to Rip's teenage years in Season 5 reveal that John helped hide the body of a murdered ranch hand whom Rip had killed by accident. In many ways, it's a scene that feels like a rite of passage between father and son, cementing the fact that Rip, not Lee, has become the rightful successor to John's cattle empire.

After nearly two seasons without hardly a passing mention of the lost Dutton, the final episode of Season 4, "Grass on the Streets and Weeds on the Rooftops," did what we never thought Yellowstone could do and brought Lee back from the grave. (Well, sort of.) As Kayce embarks on his vision quest ahead of Season 5, he sees a vision of his long-dead brother, one who wasn't particularly happy to see him. Not only did Lee confront Kayce about forgetting him, but his appearance in his brother's vision served more as a warning than a reunion. Annable's return was a welcome surprise for sure and brought the series full circle as Lee and Kayce looked eye-to-eye for the first time since the premiere.

Though it's unlikely we'll see Annable return again as Lee, it was nice to see that, after years of wondering why Sheridan and company seemed to have forgotten about him, Yellowstone was willing to elaborate further. After the death of John Dutton's latest grandson in Season 5, the patriarch recalls his own grief when Lee died back in the first season, explaining that he still blames himself to this day.

Despite being unceremoniously killed off on Yellowstone, Dave Annable has continued to play the cowboy on other neo-Westerns following his short tenure as Lee Dutton. In 2021, he landed the recurring role of Dan Miller on the CW's Walker, a modern reimagining of the original Chuck Norris-helmed Walker, Texas Ranger that took more than a few cues from Yellowstone. Appearing in 12 episodes as Miller in the midst of a Hatfield & McCoy-type family squabble, Annable has carved out a real Western legacy for himself.

Alternatively, Annable reunited with Taylor Sheridan in 2023 for Lioness. Slowly moving away from the Western genre, Sheridan's latest series is more akin to his work on the action thriller Without Remorse. After being killed off on Sheridan's flagship series, Annable was granted main cast status in the filmmaker's spy thriller. Playing Neal, the surgeon husband of Zoe Saldaña's lead character Joe, he's finally landed a role that's brought him full circle since Yellowstone. Here's hoping we'll see Annable play a cowboy again sometime soon.

A ranching family in Montana faces off against others encroaching on their land.

7 Best Oushak Rugs Fit for Royalty, Vetted by AD (2026)

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Few home furnishings can make an ordinary room feel quite so grand as Oushak rugs. Named for the Turkish town of Uşak, where the weaving tradition began, these hand-knotted floor coverings gained popularity during the Ottoman Empire and were eventually exported across Europe. Their designs are recognizable for their large-scale medallions, geometric borders, and floral motifs, many of which drew inspiration from Ottoman art and architecture. The result is a style that’s decorative but never overly fussy, which helps to explain how it has withstood the test of time.

An Oushak rug can sit comfortably beneath a traditional dining table, add some contrast to a pared-back living room, or bring some personality to an otherwise neutral bedroom. And while antique examples are certainly worth seeking out, plenty of modern Oushak rugs capture the same Old-World grandeur. Put simply, you may not be a sultan, but you can certainly decorate like one.

This one-of-a-kind, 100% wool rug is hand-knotted in Taşpinar, Turkey. Its intricate pattern, light pink palette, and low pile height of half a centimeter give it a lived-in feel that works particularly well alongside more contemporary furniture. The wool is naturally durable, so even with a little wear, the rug has plenty of life left in it.

Bring a little more floral to your floors with this lavender Oushak. Its vintage-inspired pattern is full of the botanical motifs that make Turkish rugs so distinctive, while the soft color protects its relaxed aura from feeling overly ornate. Hand-knotted from New Zealand wool, the floor covering adds just enough pattern to a room without taking over. Basically, it’s the kind of rug that makes you want to kick off your shoes and stay awhile.

This cotton-candy–hued Oushak rug, another flower-filled masterpiece, features scattered palmettes and geometric shapes that draw on the rich history of Turkish patternmaking. Handmade by artisans in India from a blend of New Zealand wool, cotton, and polyester, and available in a variety of sizes to fit many spaces, the rug’s plush feel and storied patina make it a beautiful foundation for any room.

This Oushak-inspired rug pairs symmetrical designs with a sky-blue palette, bringing brightness to any room. Think of it as artwork you can touch, or rather, walk all over, simply place it down and let the compliments roll in. It’s meticulously handwoven from high-quality wool, making it a durable choice for high-traffic areas, and because it comes in several sizes, its an especially versatile choice.

If you’re searching for a floor covering that has the essence of an antique Persian rug, but you aren’t quite ready to worry about spills, look no further. This option may feature a delicate floral pattern, but its tufted polyester construction makes it a more resilient selection for the busier corners of your home. It’s machine washable up to the 8-foot-by-10-foot size (larger versions are still washable but may require a commercial machine), and is available in area rug, round, and runner formats. It’s a particularly good pick for busy households where a beautiful rug has to do more than just look pretty.

For those who prefer more subtle patterns, this simple but sophisticated hand-knotted Oushak rug is a lovely option. Its understated mix of cream, brown, green, and blue complements a soft, slightly faded finish that gives it the appeal of a vintage find.

This Oushak rug looks like it belongs to another millennium and should live in the Metropolitan Museum of Art with its perfectly faded medallion pattern and ornamental border. But it’s actually brand new—and an affordable option for just about any home. Its muted palette can skew bohemian or coastal, or simply bring a little intrigue to a cozy space of any style.

Prince Harry and Prince William Pen Rare Letters About Their Mother to the Winners of the Diana Award

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The prize recognizes “young people who work to improve the lives of others”

One area in which Prince Harry and Prince William will always share common ground is in their respect for their late mother, Princess Diana.

Two years after Diana’s untimely passing in 1997, the Diana Award was established by former U.K. Prime Minister Gordon Brown, in an effort to recognize young people who “work to improve the lives of others.” Now, the 2026 recipients have received a special treat in the form of personalized letters from Harry and William, who expressed their congratulations, while recognizing that the winners are helping keep Diana’s legacy alive.

“You should be immensely proud of what you have achieved and the difference you are making in your community and beyond,” Harry wrote in his message. “This award is deeply personal to me, not only because it carries my mother’s name, but because it champions her belief that young people have the power to change the world. You are putting that belief into action and inspiring others in the process.”

Meanwhile, William noted: “The world we live in today can feel challenging and uncertain, and creating change is not easy. Yet, in the face of these challenges, you have shown extraordinary resilience, courage, and a belief in the power of kindness to make things better.”

The Prince of Wales also added: “My mother, in whose memory you receive the Diana Award today, would be so proud of your achievements and the deep care you show for others. Thank you for your compassionate leadership, which is helping to create a more hopeful and positive world.”

Over the past few years, the royal brothers have continued their tradition of honoring Diana Award recipients. Back in 2023, the Duke of Sussex even made an in-person visit to see some of the honorees at a Conversations for Change seminar in Los Angeles.

However, most of their efforts have been done separately, as the two continue to deal with strains in their relationship.

One of the last times the princes were seen together was back in 2021, when they attended the unveiling of a statue dedicated to their mother at the Sunken Garden in Kensington Palace. The ceremony was held on what would have been Diana’s 60th birthday.

What ‘Legend of Zelda’ Superfan Jon Batiste Thinks of ‘Ocarina of Time’ Remake and the Game’s Iconic Score

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Hey! Listen! “The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time” remake is out, and the initial response from critics is overwhelmingly positive. And while it’s still too early to tell exactly what gamers will make of the reimagined title, which released Thursday, musician Jon Batiste has offered up a teaser review as one of the lucky few superfans who got early access from Nintendo.

“I’ve gotten to play the new one, and I haven’t finished it, but you can sense that there may be some twists, some surprises that are welcome, but it’s not gonna be completely different. And that’s to its credit,” Batiste told Variety during an interview at Nintendo’s “Ocarina of Time” launch event held at the Nintendo store in New York Wednesday night. “That’s the way a melody or a musical movement, it inspires that generation and you pass it on to the next generation and they take it and take the essence of what resonates for them, but then it’s also got some other things that make it really of the now.” Related Stories Politico Plans Live Video Coverage of Midterm Elections Blue Wave Sweep: What Does Democratic Shift Mean for Hollywood and the Media?

Built exclusively for Nintendo Switch 2, “The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time” remake is based on the game released in 1998 for Nintendo 64. As in the original “Ocarina of Time” game, the remake follows Link, who must become the Hero of Time and protect the kingdom of Hyrule from the evil Ganondorf, who is plotting to steal the mythical Triforce from the Sacred Realm in hopes of ultimately taking over the world.

“That one opened my whole perception of what a game could be, and I think it did that for a lot of us,” Batiste says of playing the original 1998 release. “It feels like you’re in a universe all its own, and there’s this sort of open world that it blends things that I loved about games that I played before. Like, I love ‘Street Fighter 2’ and we love to play that because my sister and my cousins, we could have competitions and all of us could play together. But then there’s this sort of open-world RPG, but not, but action-adventure that ‘Ocarina of Time’ introduces to the canon and it makes you realize that you can blend this sort of gameplay and more.”

As a musician, Batiste naturally has an affinity for the music of “The Legend of Zelda” franchise and says he has a special fondness for the “Ocarina of Time” soundtrack from composer Koji Kondo.

“Then there’s the score,” Batiste says. “One of the things that I loved about many of the games that we played was the scores. And I started to even play those scores on instruments when I was getting to the age of us really getting into music. So this was the game that opened my mind to the possibility of what peak gameplay could be, and then you could see sort of from that point on in my choices of what I would play and what I would immerse myself in. It’s like, oh yeah, I’m trying to find that feeling again. That’s the vibe, and now it’s coming back. If it doesn’t change anything and it just captures that essence and reintroduces that to the world 20 years later, it would already be an amazing achievement.”

Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8 is $300 off ahead of Black Friday

Android fans with a bit of iPhone Duo envy, take note: The Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 is discounted to $1,599.99 at both Amazon and Best Buy, a hefty $300 markdown from its usual price, and just $50 higher than the lowest we’ve seen. Considering the device only launched a few months ago, you’re saving a significant amount of cash on the unlocked device, and you can also opt for the 512GB model, doubling the storage, for just $200 more (Amazon, Best Buy). Best Buy currently has all three colors in stock, while the 256GB model is currently sold out on Amazon in cream.

The shorter and wider passport-style shape (similar to the iPhone Duo) quickly became natural and comfortable to handle, according to our reviewer Dominic Preston’s experience with the Z Fold 8. Despite its thin and light construction, it doesn’t skimp on battery life, with a 4,800mAh battery that still had 40 percent charge remaining after a full day of use. The interior 4:3 screen has the same ratio as most Apple iPads, which is great for watching movies or TV, but even better for reading. There’s no telephoto lens on this model (it’s present on the pricier Z Fold 8 Ultra), and it only has an IP48 rating, so you’ll want to be careful near the beach, but those are small compromises to make given the discounted price.
